Benzoix/Magnific At a glance Based on observations of the southern hemisphere’s 2026 flu season, which has just ended, the upcoming season in the northern hemisphere is expected to be moderate, though predictions are difficult. Scientists are watching the emergence of small changes in H1N1 influenza viruses that could potentially influence how the coming season unfolds. Regardless of such changes, vaccination is expected to play a key role in reducing the toll of influenza in the coming months. As the nights draw in and coughs and sniffles start to spread, perennial questions are emerging about how bad this year’s flu season might be, and how well vaccines are likely to protect us. After a southern hemisphere flu season broadly in line with previous years, the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) is anticipating that its upcoming season there will be “moderate”, based on historic trends and expert opinion. However, it cautions that severity is difficult to predict before the season begins, and vaccination remains a key defence against severe disease. One development that scientists are closely monitoring is the emergence of small changes in H1N1 influenza viruses that could potentially influence how the coming season unfolds. What can we learn from the southern hemisphere’s flu season? Although influenza viruses circulate year-round, infections tend to peak during the winter months in temperate regions. Monitoring influenza activity in southern hemisphere countries such as Australia, New Zealand and South Africa, where the flu season is just ending, can therefore offer early clues about which flu viruses are circulating and what the northern hemisphere might face. Its flu season usually begins in October and can last until May. Even so, differences in population immunity, circulating flu strains and vaccination coverage mean the same patterns don’t necessarily repeat. Overall, the World Health Organization (WHO) reported fewer influenza detections globally between February and August 2026 than during the same period last year. The main viruses circulating were familiar forms of seasonal flu. These fall into two main groups: influenza A and the influenza B Victoria lineage. The former includes H1N1, a descendent of the virus that caused the 2009 swine flu pandemic, and H3N2, which has circulated in humans since 1968. Of these viruses, H3N2 was most common across the southern hemisphere, with a recently emerged subgroup known as subclade K accounting for the majority of H3N2 viruses analysed since February 2026. When Subclade K was first detected at the tail end of the southern hemisphere’s 2025 flu season, its rapid accumulation of mutations initially sparked concerns that it might evade existing immunity and vaccines. But despite the northern hemisphere’s 2025–2026 flu season starting unusually early in parts of Europe, its overall severity varied between countries, and wasn’t uniformly more severe than previous winters. Nor does this year’s southern hemisphere flu season appear to have been unusually severe overall. “Within the variation of flu seasons, it wasn’t really anything to write home about,” said Dr Ruth Harvey, Deputy Director of the Worldwide Influenza Centre at the Francis Crick Institute in London. What could this mean for the northern hemisphere? While the southern hemisphere’s experience offers some reassurance, it doesn’t guarantee that northern hemisphere countries will face a similar flu season. Speaking at a WHO meeting on influenza vaccine composition in late September, Prof Nicola Lewis, Director of the Worldwide Influenza Centre at the Francis Crick Institute, said: “In recent weeks, we’ve seen the continued circulation of influenza, perhaps to higher levels than we saw at the same time last year in 2025, and we’ve also continued to see both influenza A and influenza B.” One development that scientists are watching is a recent increase in detections of H1N1 influenza viruses. Last year influenza vaccines prevented an estimated 180,000 hospitalisations and thousands of deaths in the US alone. Within a subgroup group of these viruses, known as D.3.1.1, researchers have identified small changes in the haemagglutinin protein found on the viruses’ surface. Laboratory tests have suggested that antibodies may recognise some of these variants less well than the H1N1 virus targeted by this winter’s flu vaccine, although it is currently unclear whether this will translate into reduced protection. Researchers at the Francis Crick Institute are currently running further tests using a technique that measures how well antibodies from large numbers of people with different infection and vaccination histories recognise emerging flu viruses. This approach helped reveal that people had greater pre-existing immunity against last winter’s H3N2 subclade K variant that earlier laboratory studies had suggested, and that vaccination boosted these responses. Have you read? New study suggests severe flu can damage the heart Why alcohol-based hand sanitiser doesn’t kill norovirus and five other winter illness myths Should I get the seasonal flu vaccine? Vaccination remains a key means of preventing serious flu-related illness, hospitalisation and death, particularly among people who are most vulnerable to complications. Last year influenza vaccines prevented an estimated 180,000 hospitalisations and thousands of deaths in the US alone. This year’s Northern Hemisphere flu vaccines target H1N1, H3N2 and influenza B viruses, and were updated earlier this year to better match recently circulating strains. They are expected to play an important role in reducing the toll of influenza in the coming months, so it is worth taking up the offer of a seasonal flu vaccine if you’re eligible for one. Time
The southern hemisphere’s flu season is ending now.
Location and population
Southern hemisphere countries such as Australia, New Zealand, and South Africa. Northern hemisphere countries.
Event
The southern hemisphere’s flu season is expected to be moderate, though predictions are difficult. Scientists are monitoring small changes in H1N1 influenza viruses that could influence the upcoming season. The main viruses circulating were familiar forms of seasonal flu, including H1N1 and the influenza B Victoria lineage. The WHO reported fewer influenza detections globally between February and August 2026 than during the same period last year.
Scale, impact and risk
The upcoming northern hemisphere flu season is expected to be moderate. The severity is difficult to predict before the season begins. Vaccination is expected to play a key role in reducing the toll of influenza. H3N2 was most common in the southern hemisphere, with subclade K accounting for the majority of H3N2 viruses analysed since February 2026.
Response, status and uncertainty
Researchers are running further tests to assess how well antibodies from people with different infection and vaccination histories recognize emerging flu viruses. The WHO and CDC are monitoring the situation and providing guidance. The severity of the upcoming season is difficult to predict, and vaccination remains a key defense against severe disease.

FluView 第40周:抗病毒药物 抗药性标记在H1N1流感病毒中的夏季出现 - 禽流感日记

FluView Week 40: Antiviral Resistance Markers in H1N1 Over The Summer - Avian Flu Diary
新闻简报禽流感日记报告于2026年10月19日发布,强调了季节性流感流感病毒中出现抗病毒药物抗药性标记的情况,尤其是H1N1和H3N2病毒。在H1N1病毒中,I223V和S247N 突变首次于2023年在香港报告,并在2026年时在西班牙和美国显著增加。在美国,2024-2025流感季节中,对517个H1N1分离株进行测试,发现其中10个表现出对奥司他韦抑制作用的降低,4个因H275Y 突变表现出高度降低的抑制作用。到2026年夏季,美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)报告称有8个H1N1病毒携带I223V和S247N 突变,与前一年相比增加了35倍。尽管总体数量仍较低,但报告指出需要持续监测,因为这些突变可能影响抗病毒药物抗病毒药物的效果。这些发现基于《柳叶刀·微生物》、《EID期刊》、《病毒研究》和《欧洲流行病学监测》等研究,其中CDC提供了最新的数据。报告还指出,近期美国分离株中未发现H275Y 突变,这令人安心,但并不能消除对抗药性模式演变的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告于2026年10月19日星期一发布。事件日期范围从2024年3月到2026年2月下旬,最新的数据来自2026年夏季。
地点与对象
证据覆盖全球和区域地点,包括香港、西班牙、加泰罗尼亚和美国。受影响的人群包括感染季节性流感病毒的个体,尤其是H1N1和H3N2病毒。
事件
该报告讨论了季节性H1N1和H3N2流感病毒中出现的抗病毒药物抗药性标记,特别是H1N1中的I223V和S247N 突变,以及H3N2中的E119V 突变。这些突变会降低对奥司他韦和帕拉米韦的敏感性。这些发现基于发表在《柳叶刀·微生物》、《EID期刊》、《病毒研究》和《欧洲流行病学监测》上的研究。
规模、影响与风险
在2024-2025流感季节,美国对517个H1N1分离株进行测试,发现其中10个表现出对奥司他韦抑制作用的降低,4个因H275Y 突变表现出高度降低的抑制作用。在2025-2026流感季节,报告了20个抑制作用降低的结果和11个高度抑制作用降低的结果。2026年夏季,CDC报告称有8个H1N1病毒携带I223V和S247N 突变。与前一年相比,S247N 突变增加了35倍,2026年夏季H1N1病毒中携带S247N+I223V 突变的比例为3.1%,而2026年5月则为2.1%。
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC和其他卫生组织一直在监测流感病毒中的抗病毒药物抗药性。报告指出,这些突变的存在是一个令人担忧的趋势,但其对公共卫生的影响仍不明确。这些发现基于检测和监测数据,对于这些突变的全部影响仍存在一些不确定性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

FluView Week 40: Antiviral Resistance Markers in H1N1 Over The Summer - Avian Flu Diary

News Brief
The Avian Flu Diary report, published on October 19, 2026, highlights the emergence of antiviral resistance markers in seasonal influenza viruses, particularly H1N1 and H3N2. The I223V and S247N mutations in H1N1 were first reported in Hong Kong in 2023 and showed a significant increase in Spain and the U.S. by 2026. In the U.S., during the 2024-2025 flu season, 10 out of 517 H1N1 isolates tested exhibited reduced oseltamivir inhibition, and 4 showed highly reduced inhibition due to the H275Y mutation. By the summer of 2026, the CDC reported 8 H1N1 viruses with the I223V and S247N mutations, marking a 35-fold increase compared to the previous year. While the overall numbers remain low, the report underscores the need for continued monitoring as these mutations could impact the effectiveness of antiviral drugs. The findings are based on studies from The Lancet Microbe, EID Journal, Virus Research, and Eurosurveillance, with the CDC providing the most recent data. The report also notes the absence of H275Y mutations in recent U.S. isolates, which is reassuring but does not eliminate concerns about the evolving resistance patterns.
Original source text (English)
Avian Flu Diary Covering Pandemic and Seasonal Flu, Emerging Infectious Diseases, public health, community & Individual preparedness. NOTE: All AFD blogs are written by a human - any mistakes are solely mine. Monday, October 19, 2026 FluView Week 40: Antiviral Resistance Markers in H1N1 Over The Summer #19,379 For the past 3 years we've been following scattered reports of `reduced susceptibility' of the seasonal H1N1 flu virus to the antiviral drug oseltamivir (aka `Tamiflu') around the globe. In March 2024 The Lancet Microbe published - Global Emergence of Neuraminidase Inhibitor-Resistant Influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 Viruses with I223V and S247N Mutations - which reported reduced oseltamivir inhibition among samples tested in Hong Kong in 2023. Unlike the H275Y mutation - which caused nearly total resistance in an older (now extinct) strain of H1N1 back in 2008 - these viruses carried dual I223V/S247N mutations which together produced a modest ≈ 10-fold reduced inhibition by oseltamivir. A concerning trend, but not nearly enough of a reduction to invalidate the use of the drug. Three months later (June 2024), the EID Journal: Multicountry Spread of Influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 Viruses with Reduced Oseltamivir Inhibition, May 2023–February 2024 reported a ≈ 13-fold reduced inhibition by oseltamivir. In July of 2025, Virus Research: A 15-year Study of Neuraminidase Mutations and the Increasing of S247N Mutation in Spain, we looked at a study that found a sharp increase in detections of the NA:S247N mutation beginning in 2024, but not combined with I223V. Last December, in Eurosurveillance: Expansion of influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 NA:S247N Viruses with Reduced Susceptibility to Oseltamivir, Catalonia, Spain, and in Europe, July to October 2025. a Rapid Communications reported on another dramatic surge in H1N1 viruses carrying the NA:S247N mutation in Catalonia Spain, and other parts of Europe. Abrupt rise in resistance in Catalonia, Spain - fall 2025 Some weeks (see above graphic), as many as 100% of viruses tested showed this NA:S247N mutation. The good news, however, was that once again a second synergistic mutation - NA:I223V - was not detected in these recent Catalonia isolates. As for detections in the United States (see Feb 2026 blog), over the entire 2024-2025 flu season - out of 1697 H1N1 viruses tested - only one carried the NA-I223V and NA-S247N amino acid substitutions. During the first 13 weeks of the 2025-2026 flu season (Oct - Dec) the CDC reported zero elevated resistance among the first 193 H1N1 viruses tested (see FluView Wk 53). But shortly after the New Year we began to seen an uptick in reduced inhibition detections in the United States. By late February (FluView week 7) - based on 517 H1N1 isolates tested since October - the CDC reported 10 isolates with reduced inhibition and 4 with highly reduced inhibition (due to NA:H275Y). In early June we looked at the last full FluView report of the 2025-2026 flu season, which reported a total 20 reduced inhibition results, and 11 highly reduced results: Nine A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses had NA-H275Y amino acid substitution conferring highly reduced inhibition by oseltamivir and peramivir. Nineteen A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses had amino acid substitutions NA-I223V and NA-S247N and showed reduced inhibition by oseltamivir. One A(H1N1)pdm09 virus had amino acid substitutions NA-I223T and NA-S247N and showed reduced inhibition by oseltamivir. Two A(H3N2) viruses had amino acid substitution NA-E119V conferring highly reduced inhibition by oseltamivir. Three B viruses had amino acid substitution NA-M464T and showed reduced inhibition by peramivir. While the number of S247N+I223V mutations remained relatively low (2.1%), this was more than a 35-fold increase over the previous year. And equally concerning - after an extended absence - the tag team of S247N+I223V was being reported again in a newer (D.3.x) NA-clade H1N1 virus. With the release last Friday of the first full FluView report since last May, we've our first look at the reported detections of the S247N+I223V amino acid substitution over the summer. While the overall numbers are low - and the lack of any reported H275Y mutations is reassuring - the percentage of H1N1 viruses that harbor the S247N+I223V mutation was nearly 50% higher (3.1%) than at the end of May (2.1%). Viruses collected in the U.S. since May 24, 2026, were tested for antiviral susceptibility as follows: Eight A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses had amino acid substitutions NA-I223V and NA-S247N and showed reduced inhibition by oseltamivir. By way of comparison, over the summer of 2025 the CDC reported zero detections, and none were reported until early 2026. It still isn't clear how much of a public health impact these mutations will have - if any - going forward, but this week's FluView report provides a useful benchmark of where things stand as we enter into the 2026-2027 flu season. Stay tuned. Things could get interesting. Sunday, October 18, 2026 WHO DON: Middle East respiratory syndrome coronavirus - Kingdom of Saudi Arabia Photo Credit NIAID #19,378 While it doesn't get near the attention it did before COVID, MERS-CoV continues to circulate in dromedary camels in the Middle East, occasionally spills over into humans, and is likely under reported. MERS-CoV also continues to evolve, which raises concerns over its future impact (see Health Sci Rpts (Narrative Review): Pathogenicity and Potential Role of MERS-CoV in the Emergence of “Disease X”). In 2025, the WHO released 3 MERS-CoV DON reports (see here, here, and here), and last December, France reported 2 Travelers Returning From Arabian Peninsula Diagnosed with the MERS-CoV virus. This year has been somewhat quieter, with the first WHO update of 2026 published just last week (October 15th), which chronicles 8 cases reported by Saudi Arabia since last December. Of note - while the WHO states `Humans contract MERS-CoV through direct or indirect contact with dromedary camels, the virus’s natural host and zoonotic reservoir.' - 6 of the 8 cases reported no known contact with camels or camel products. This is not a new trend; in 2025 more than half of the cases reported by KSA listed no known contact with camels or camel products, and this has been frequently reported in the past. Exactly how these people are contracting the virus remains unclear. First some excerpts from the latest DON report, after which I'll have a postscript. Middle East respiratory syndrome coronavirus - Kingdom of Saudi Arabia 15 October 2026 Situation at a glance Between 1 January and 5 October 2026, the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia reported eight laboratory-confirmed cases of Middle East Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus (MERS-CoV) infection, including one death. Cases were detected across six locations: Al Jouf (1), Arar (1), Hafr Al-Batin (1), Riyadh (2), Tabuk (1) and Taif (2). No epidemiological links were identified between any of the cases. All eight cases were symptomatic, most commonly presenting with fever (6/8), dyspnea (5/8), and cough (4/8). None were health and care workers. Infection was confirmed by RT-PCR testing. MERS is a zoonotic viral respiratory disease for which dromedary camels serve as the primary animal reservoir and two cases reported direct contact with camels while six reported no known camel exposure. Seven of eight cases had underlying comorbidities, recognized risk factors for severe MERS-CoV disease. The cases appear to represent sporadic infections related to zoonotic spillover rather than a single outbreak cluster. Description of the situation Between 1 January and 5 October 2026, a total of eight cases of MERS-CoV, including one death, was reported from six locations in the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia. Of the eight reported cases, five occurred among persons aged 70 years and older, two among adults aged 50–59 years, and one among an adult aged 30–39 years. Seven cases had underlying comorbidities, two reported direct camel contact, and one death was reported in an older adult (>65 year old) with comorbidities. There was no known epidemiological link identified among cases. Case 1: A male aged 50–59 years, with comorbidities, from Al Jouf developed symptoms on 14 March 2026. He reported no known contact with camels or camel products. The patient was hospitalized on 17 March and laboratory confirmation of MERS-CoV infection was obtained by RT-PCR on 19 March. Case 2: A male aged ≥70 years, with comorbidities, from Taif developed symptoms on 18 April 2026. He reported no known contact with camels or camel products. The patient was hospitalized on the same day, and laboratory confirmation was obtained on 22 April. He subsequently died as a result of the illness. Case 3: A male aged ≥70 years, with comorbidities, from Hafr Al-Batin developed symptoms on 20 May 2026. He reported direct contact with camels. The patient was hospitalized on 26 May and laboratory confirmation was obtained on 27 May. Case 4: A female aged ≥70 years, with comorbidities, from Riyadh developed symptoms on 31 July 2026. No known exposure to camels or camel products was reported. She was hospitalized on 3 August and laboratory confirmation was obtained on 8 August. Case 5: A male aged 50–59 years, with comorbidities, from Tabuk developed symptoms on 20 August 2026. He reported no known contact with camels or camel products. He was hospitalized on 23 August and laboratory confirmation was obtained by RT-PCR on 24 August. Case 6: A male aged 30–39 years, with no known comorbidities, from Riyadh developed symptoms on 14 August 2026. He reported no known contact with camels or camel products. He was hospitalized on 20 August and laboratory confirmation was obtained on 23 August. Case 7: A male aged ≥70 years, with comorbidities, from Taif developed symptoms on 11 September 2026. He reported no known contact with camels or camel products. He was hospitalized on 17 September and laboratory confirmation was obtained on 19 September. Case 8: A male aged 60–69 years, with comorbidities, from Arar developed symptoms on 20 September 2026. He reported direct contact with camels. He was hospitalized on 20 September and laboratory confirmation was obtained on 21 September. (SNIP) WHO risk assessment MERS is a zoonotic viral respiratory disease caused by MERS-CoV, for which dromedary camels serve as the primary animal reservoir. Human infection can range from asymptomatic or mild illness to severe disease and death. Human infections continue to occur through direct or indirect exposure to infected dromedaries or their products, while limited human-to-human transmission has been documented, primarily among close contacts and in healthcare settings. Sustained community transmission has not been observed. No licensed vaccine for MERS-CoV exists to date. As of 5 October 2026, WHO had been notified of 2643 laboratory-confirmed MERS cases globally, including 965 deaths, since the virus was first identified in 2012. The majority of cases (84%; 2232 cases and 870 deaths) have been reported from Saudi Arabia. These figures likely underestimate the true number of infections since mild and asymptomatic cases may remain undetected/unreported, while the reported case fatality of ratio of 37% may overestimate mortality among infections. Although the number of reported MERS cases has declined substantially since 2020, MERS-CoV continues to circulate in dromedary camel populations in the Middle East and African regions and continues to cause sporadic human infections. The observed decline in reported MERS cases, in particular during the COVID-19 pandemic emergency phase, is thought to be a result of pandemic-related IPC measures that also limited human-to-human transmission of MERS-CoV, as well as behavioral changes during the pandemic. Any role of potential cross-reactive immunity from SARS-CoV-2 infection and/ or vaccination remains in need of further investigation. Other hypotheses—such as reduced surveillance, viral attenuation, or decreased circulation in camel populations—are not supported by current evidence. WHO expects additional sporadic MERS cases to occur in countries where the virus circulates in camels and anticipates that travel-associated exportation of cases will continue. Delays in detection remain possible due to the clinical presentation of MERS being similar with other respiratory infections, including influenza and COVID-19. Numerous outbreaks in KSA and the Republic of Korea have demonstrated that primary cases can result in substantial secondary transmission, particularly in healthcare settings and through importation of travel related cases. The notification of the recent cases in Saudi Arabia does not change WHO's overall risk assessment - the risk remains moderate at both the regional and global levels, reflecting the continued occurrence of sporadic zoonotic infections, the potential for healthcare-associated outbreaks and international exportation of cases, in the absence of sustained human-to-human transmission. WHO continues to monitor the epidemiological situation through routine surveillance and reporting under the International Health Regulations (IHR 2005) and conducts ongoing risk assessments using available epidemiological and virological information. (Continue . . . ) In June of 2025 we looked at an editorial in Journal of Epidemiology & Global Health on the importance of healthcare preparedness and surveillance (see Al-Tawfiq & Memish On Recurrent MERS-CoV Transmission in Saudi Arabia), with an emphasis on identifying asymptomatic carriers. Two months later we reviewed an IJID Editorial: Al-Tawfiq on Global Epidemiology and Public Health Challenges of Middle East Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus (MERS-CoV) - which looked again at what may be the most challenging aspect of MERS detection; asymptomatic or mildly symptomatic cases. Although MERS-CoV clearly isn't ready for prime time, each new zoonotic spillover from camels - or cryptic transmission - is another opportunity for the virus to better adapt to a human host. Which is why we need better MERS-CoV surveillance and reporting, both in the Middle East, and in Africa (see EID Journal: Geographic Distribution of MERS-CoV among Dromedary Camels, Africa). Saturday, October 17, 2026 Washington State Confirms 3rd Dairy Herd Infected With H5N1 #19,377 Since 2024 Washington State has seen scores of HPAI outbreaks in poultry, and more than a dozen confirmed and probable human infections related to exposure to poultry and/or wild birds. Eight days ago (Oct 9th), however, their WSDA confirmed their first detection of avian influenza in dairy cattle (herds=2). Yesterday, Washington State reported their first (reportedly mild) human infection with H5N1 derived from an (unspecified) dairy exposure. Details on this latest case remain scant, and yesterday's CDC FluView Report (wk 40) did not include this case, reporting: Novel Influenza A Virus Infections No new confirmed human infections with a novel influenza virus were reported to CDC as of October 14, 2026. The CDC's A(H5) Bird Flu: Current Situation webpage was updated on the 15th, showing the latest dairy-associated case (see below). Presumably we'll get an update in next week's FluView. In the meantime, the USDA has added a 3rd dairy herd from Washington State to their HPAI positive list. Other than the one-line entry (below), I've seen no other information on this outbreak. Thus far, the genotype of the virus infecting Washington Cattle (likely either B3.13 or D1.1) - and the recent human case - have not been announced, nor do we know exactly what kind of exposure the recent human case had. For more than 2 years the CDC, the USDA, and state agricultural agencies have urged the use of PPEs for farm workers, and anyone else who might be exposed to infected livestock or birds. While recommended, the use of PPEs has been suboptimal, particularly on farms and ranches. They can be hot, cumbersome to wear, and difficult to don and doff properly. (see MMWR: PPE Use by Dairy Farmworkers Exposed to Cows Infected with HPAI A(H5N1) Viruses — Colorado, 2024). Over the past 30 days the number of infected backyard flocks vs. commercial flocks has run nearly 2 to 1, and over the course of the past 4+ years the number of backyard flock (n=1227) outbreaks have exceeded commercial outbreaks (n=1048). Although the risks of infection are believed low, we've already seen at least two fatalities (here, and here) in the United States from H5Nx following `. . . exposure to a combination of a non-commercial backyard flock and wild birds.' The APPA estimates `Eleven (11) million U.S. households own backyard chickens (a 28% increase from 2023)', yet a recent survey (see MMWR: Knowledge, Attitudes, and Practices Regarding Avian Influenza Among Owners of Backyard Flocks) found significant gaps in their knowledge of dealing with avian flu. Although it is too early to predict what kind of avian flu season we will see this fall and winter - or whether any new genotypes will be added to the mix - we are already seeing signs of increased activity. Which means, if you raise backyard birds, frequent live markets, or have outdoor pets, now would be a good time to familiarize yourselves with the risks of avian flu, and the things you can do to reduce those risks to you and your families. UF/IFAS Extension: What Backyard Flock Owners Need to Know about Bird Flu (Influenza H5N1) CDC Guidance on Bird Flu in Pets and Other Animals Friday, October 16, 2026 Washington State Confirms Human Avian Flu Infection #19,376 A week ago Washington state confirmed their first detection of avian influenza in dairy cattle, and overnight it was announced by the Yakima County Health District that a local resident who was exposed to infected dairy cows has tested positive for the virus. There is still a lot we don't know about this case, hopefully the CDC will provide more details later today in their weekly FluView. The full press release follows. I'll up...
View five news elements
Time
The report was published on Monday, October 19, 2026. The event dates span from March 2024 to late February 2026, with the most recent data from the summer of 2026.
Location and population
The evidence covers global and regional locations, including Hong Kong, Spain, Catalonia, and the United States. The affected population includes individuals infected with seasonal influenza viruses, particularly H1N1 and H3N2.
Event
The report discusses the emergence of antiviral resistance markers in seasonal H1N1 and H3N2 influenza viruses, specifically the I223V and S247N mutations in H1N1, and the E119V mutation in H3N2. These mutations reduce susceptibility to oseltamivir and peramivir. The findings are based on studies published in The Lancet Microbe, EID Journal, Virus Research, and Eurosurveillance.
Scale, impact and risk
In the 2024-2025 flu season, 10 out of 517 H1N1 isolates tested in the U.S. showed reduced inhibition by oseltamivir, and 4 showed highly reduced inhibition due to the H275Y mutation. In the 2025-2026 flu season, 20 reduced inhibition results and 11 highly reduced results were reported. In the summer of 2026, the CDC reported 8 H1N1 viruses with the I223V and S247N mutations. The S247N mutation showed a 35-fold increase compared to the previous year, and the percentage of H1N1 viruses with the S247N+I223V mutation was 3.1% in the summer of 2026, up from 2.1% in May 2026.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC and other health organizations have been monitoring antiviral resistance in influenza viruses. The report notes that the presence of these mutations is a concerning trend, but the impact on public health remains unclear. The findings are based on testing and surveillance data, with some uncertainty about the full implications of these mutations.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

美国流感季提前开始,病例激增 - KOA 850 AM & 94.1 FM

Flu Season Begins Early With Rising Cases In The U.S. - KOA 850 AM & 94.1 FM
新闻简报文章报道,美国流感季比往常提前开始,美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)指出自9月初以来流感病例显著增加。根据《美国今日》报道,全国流感检测阳性率目前为3.6%,比去年同期高出六倍。涵盖加利福尼亚州、亚利桑那州、内华达州和夏威夷州的地区报告了最高的阳性率,达到9.5%,通常出现在流感季后期。CDC的FluView报告指出,全国范围内检测的呼吸道样本中有5%呈流感阳性,其中流感甲型H1N1流感病毒pdm09是主要流行毒株。这种亚型曾导致2009年猪流感大流行,目前导致超过96%的感染。卫生专家敦促疫苗接种现在接种疫苗,因为流感疫苗大约需要两周时间才能生效。CDC建议所有6个月及以上的人都应接种疫苗,尤其是那些感染后更容易出现严重疾病的高风险人群,如幼儿、老年人以及患有慢性健康问题的人。最佳接种时间应是在10月底之前,以确保在流感活动达到高峰时获得充分保护。除了疫苗接种,卫生专家还建议改善室内通风、保持良好的手部卫生以及生病时留在家中,以降低传播风险。如果症状变得严重,建议及时就医。
查看新闻五要素
时间
美国流感季比往常提前开始,美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)报告自9月初以来流感病例显著上升(N2)。目前全国流感检测阳性率为3.6%(N3)。涵盖加利福尼亚州、亚利桑那州、内华达州和夏威夷州的地区报告了最高的阳性率,达到9.5%(N4)。CDC的FluView报告指出,全国范围内检测的呼吸道样本中有5%呈流感阳性(N6)。
地点与对象
CDC报告全国流感检测阳性率为3.6%(N3)。
事件
CDC的FluView报告指出,全国范围内检测的呼吸道样本中有5%呈流感阳性,其中流感甲型H1N1流感病毒pdm09为主要流行毒株(N6)。
规模、影响与风险
目前全国流感检测阳性率为3.6%,比去年同期高出六倍(N3)。涵盖加利福尼亚州、亚利桑那州、内华达州和夏威夷州的地区报告了最高的阳性率,达到9.5%,通常在11月或12月才会出现这一水平(N4)。卫生专家强调现在接种疫苗的重要性,因为流感疫苗大约需要两周时间才能生效(N5)。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生专家建议改善室内通风、保持良好的手部卫生以及生病时留在家中,以降低流感传播风险(N12)。CDC建议所有6个月及以上的人都应接种流感疫苗,尤其是那些感染后更容易出现严重疾病的高风险人群,如幼儿、老年人以及患有慢性健康问题的人(N10)。最佳接种时间应是在10月底之前,以确保在流感活动通常达到高峰时获得充分保护(N11)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Flu Season Begins Early With Rising Cases In The U.S. - KOA 850 AM & 94.1 FM

News Brief
The article reports that the flu season has begun earlier than usual in the United States, with the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) noting a significant increase in influenza cases since early September. The nationwide positivity rate for influenza is currently 3.6%, which is six times higher than the same period last year, according to USA Today. The region encompassing California, Arizona, Nevada, and Hawaii has reported the highest positivity rate at 9.5%, typically seen later in the season. The CDC's FluView report indicates that 5% of respiratory specimens tested nationally were positive for influenza, with influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 being the dominant strain. This subtype, responsible for the 2009 swine flu pandemic, is causing over 96% of current infections. Health experts are urging vaccination now, as it takes about two weeks for the flu shot to become effective. The CDC recommends that everyone aged six months and older get vaccinated, especially those at higher risk of severe illness, such as young children, the elderly, and individuals with chronic health conditions. The ideal time for vaccination is by the end of October to ensure protection when flu activity peaks. In addition to vaccination, health experts advise improving indoor ventilation, practicing good hand hygiene, and staying home when sick to reduce transmission risk. If symptoms become severe, prompt medical attention is recommended.
Original source text (English)
___ Play Now Log InSign Up National News Flu Season Begins Early With Rising Cases In The U.S. By iHeartRadio Oct 19, 2026 Photo: Getty Images North America Flu season has started earlier than usual in the United States, with the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reporting a significant rise in influenza cases since early September. The nationwide positivity rate for influenza is currently 3.6%, which is six times higher than the same period last year, according to USA Today. The region encompassing California, Arizona, Nevada, and Hawaii has reported the highest positivity rate at 9.5%, a level typically not seen until November or December. Health experts emphasize the importance of getting vaccinated now, as it takes about two weeks for the flu shot to become effective. The CDC's FluView report indicates that 5% of respiratory specimens tested nationally were positive for influenza, with influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 being the predominant strain. This subtype, which was responsible for the 2009 "swine flu" pandemic, is causing over 96% of current infections. The early rise in flu activity has prompted concerns about the potential for a severe flu season. Forbes reports that influenza viruses are constantly evolving, and mutations may allow them to evade immunity from previous infections and vaccinations. The CDC recommends that everyone aged six months and older receive a flu vaccine, especially those at higher risk of severe illness, such as young children, the elderly, and individuals with chronic health conditions. The ideal time to get vaccinated is by the end of October to ensure full protection when flu activity typically peaks. In addition to vaccination, health experts advise improving indoor ventilation, practicing good hand hygiene, and staying home when sick to reduce the risk of flu transmission. If flu symptoms become severe, medical attention should be sought promptly. 上海艾克生物制药获得美国FDA IND许可,推进其长效流感预防药物AK0406的II期临床试验 - BioPharma APAC

Shanghai Ark Biopharmaceutical Secures US FDA IND Clearance for Phase II Trial of Long-Acting Influenza Prevention Drug AK0406 - BioPharma APAC
新闻简报Shanghai Ark Biopharmaceutical has secured U.S. FDA IND clearance for a phase II clinical trial of its long-acting influenza prevention drug AK0406. This development comes as the 2025–2026 influenza season in the United States resulted in at least 32 million illnesses, 380,000 hospitalizations, and 24,000 deaths, underscoring the need for alternative preventive strategies. The trial targets the general population, with a focus on vulnerable groups such as older adults and immunocompromised individuals, who may experience limited vaccine protection due to changes in circulating influenza viruses and variability in immune responses. The drug is a long-acting antiviral drug-Fc conjugate (AFC), designed to provide extended protection against seasonal influenza. The FDA's clearance allows the company to proceed with the trial, which aims to address unmet needs in influenza prevention. The evidence highlights the significance of this trial in the context of ongoing public health challenges related to seasonal influenza.

规模、影响与风险
The 2025–2026 influenza season in the U.S. resulted in at least 32 million illnesses, 380,000 hospitalizations, and 24,000 deaths, highlighting the need for additional preventive approaches.

Shanghai Ark Biopharmaceutical Secures US FDA IND Clearance for Phase II Trial of Long-Acting Influenza Prevention Drug AK0406 - BioPharma APAC

News Brief
Shanghai Ark Biopharmaceutical has secured U.S. FDA IND clearance for a phase II clinical trial of its long-acting influenza prevention drug AK0406. This development comes as the 2025–2026 influenza season in the United States resulted in at least 32 million illnesses, 380,000 hospitalizations, and 24,000 deaths, underscoring the need for alternative preventive strategies. The trial targets the general population, with a focus on vulnerable groups such as older adults and immunocompromised individuals, who may experience limited vaccine protection due to changes in circulating influenza viruses and variability in immune responses. The drug is a long-acting antiviral drug-Fc conjugate (AFC), designed to provide extended protection against seasonal influenza. The FDA's clearance allows the company to proceed with the trial, which aims to address unmet needs in influenza prevention. The evidence highlights the significance of this trial in the context of ongoing public health challenges related to seasonal influenza.

Shanghai Ark Biopharmaceutical announced that the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has granted its investigational new drug (IND) application for AK0406, a long-acting antiviral drug-Fc conjugate (AFC), enabling the company to initiate a phase II clinical trial for influenza prevention in general population in the United States. Addressing Gaps in Influenza Prevention Seasonal vaccination remains central to influenza prevention. However, changes in circulating influenza viruses and variability in individual immune response can limit vaccine protection, particularly among older adults and people who are immunocompromised. Additional preventive approaches could help address these unmet needs. According to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), despite the availability of influenza vaccines, the 2025–2026 influenza season resulted in at least 32 million illnesses, 380,000 hospitalizations and 24,000 deaths in the United States.
